========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.

Like with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.

And with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.

Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.

I do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.

So… I’m not sure. Maybe not hopeless, but I don’t have a very clear feeling of looking forward right now.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has gone quiet. Now I just don’t really feel drawn toward any of it. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. I’m still doing things, mostly because they’re obligations or routines, but I don’t feel pulled toward them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Yes… I think so. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.

It’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has gone quiet. Like I still do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s mostly obligations.\n\nI used to get something out of my work, and yoga, and going to galleries—there’s this small gallery near me that used to change the whole feel of my week—and even journaling. Now I just don’t really feel drawn toward any of it. It’s like I’m moving through the motions, and I don’t know, that’s been hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and everything shifted—space, routines, even the way I think about work and people. I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment, you know, like when furniture is in the wrong place and you keep bumping into it, but it’s been… yeah, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like spending time with friends or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.\n\nAnd with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that. Sometimes I get tired in this heavy way, like I wish I could just not have to deal with everything for a while, but that’s different, I think.\n\nIt’s funny, “death” makes me think of this client I had years ago who used to talk around it without ever saying the word, and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what they meant—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess… no clear thoughts of wanting to die that I’ve noticed. More like wanting things to pause.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but that’s not really the thing I notice most. I mean, sometimes I’m slower getting started, like I’ll sit with my journal open and not write anything, or I’ll take forever to leave for yoga even though I know the class time.\n\nBut what finally made me come in is more that the part of me that used to reach for people, and enjoy my work, and yoga, and galleries… that part feels very quiet. I’m still doing things, mostly because they’re obligations or routines, but I don’t feel pulled toward them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. My scale at home is one of those old ones that kind of gives a different answer depending on where you stand, which is annoying—I bought it years ago after a client was talking about calorie counting and it made me think I should be more “aware,” but then it just became another object in the bathroom.\n\nSorry. In terms of weight, I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting very differently or anything dramatic. So… no significant loss that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.\n\nI do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe not hopeless, but I don’t have a very clear feeling of looking forward right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is worse than your feelings of sadness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.\n\nIt’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. Like I’m still showing up to things because they’re on the calendar, or because someone expects me to, but it feels like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting them.\n\nI remember I used to plan weekends around exhibits, and I’d have opinions about the lighting or the way things were hung—probably too many opinions, honestly. Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So yes, I’d say the lack of interest is more noticeable than sadness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A few, maybe, but not in the way I used to. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.

I used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.

So I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.

So… no significant loss that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.\n\nI do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe not hopeless, but I don’t have a very clear feeling of looking forward right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is worse than your feelings of sadness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.\n\nIt’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. Like I’m still showing up to things because they’re on the calendar, or because someone expects me to, but it feels like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting them.\n\nI remember I used to plan weekends around exhibits, and I’d have opinions about the lighting or the way things were hung—probably too many opinions, honestly. Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So yes, I’d say the lack of interest is more noticeable than sadness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A few, maybe, but not in the way I used to. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.

What finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.

I keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. So… yes, I’d say it’s been getting worse enough that I noticed I’m mostly just moving through obligations now.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has gone quiet. Like I still do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s mostly obligations.\n\nI used to get something out of my work, and yoga, and going to galleries—there’s this small gallery near me that used to change the whole feel of my week—and even journaling. Now I just don’t really feel drawn toward any of it. It’s like I’m moving through the motions, and I don’t know, that’s been hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and everything shifted—space, routines, even the way I think about work and people. I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment, you know, like when furniture is in the wrong place and you keep bumping into it, but it’s been… yeah, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like spending time with friends or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.\n\nAnd with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. Guilty… I’m not sure. Not about one clear thing, I don’t think.

I mean, I can feel bad that I’m not as present as I used to be, especially with people who probably expect me to show up in a certain way. But then I start thinking about how much of my work has always been about being present for other people, and how strange it is when you can do that professionally—or at least try to—and then your own life feels sort of… unattended.

But guilt about something specific? No, nothing really comes to mind. It’s more vague than that, if it’s there at all.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has gone quiet. Like I still do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s mostly obligations.\n\nI used to get something out of my work, and yoga, and going to galleries—there’s this small gallery near me that used to change the whole feel of my week—and even journaling. Now I just don’t really feel drawn toward any of it. It’s like I’m moving through the motions, and I don’t know, that’s been hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and everything shifted—space, routines, even the way I think about work and people. I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment, you know, like when furniture is in the wrong place and you keep bumping into it, but it’s been… yeah, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like spending time with friends or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.\n\nAnd with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that. Sometimes I get tired in this heavy way, like I wish I could just not have to deal with everything for a while, but that’s different, I think.\n\nIt’s funny, “death” makes me think of this client I had years ago who used to talk around it without ever saying the word, and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what they meant—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess… no clear thoughts of wanting to die that I’ve noticed. More like wanting things to pause.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but that’s not really the thing I notice most. I mean, sometimes I’m slower getting started, like I’ll sit with my journal open and not write anything, or I’ll take forever to leave for yoga even though I know the class time.\n\nBut what finally made me come in is more that the part of me that used to reach for people, and enjoy my work, and yoga, and galleries… that part feels very quiet. I’m still doing things, mostly because they’re obligations or routines, but I don’t feel pulled toward them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? In terms of weight, I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting very differently or anything dramatic. So… no significant loss that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.\n\nI do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. So… yes, I’d say it’s been getting worse enough that I noticed I’m mostly just moving through obligations now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. Not about one clear thing, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I can feel bad that I’m not as present as I used to be, especially with people who probably expect me to show up in a certain way. But then I start thinking about how much of my work has always been about being present for other people, and how strange it is when you can do that professionally—or at least try to—and then your own life feels sort of… unattended.\n\nBut guilt about something specific? No, nothing really comes to mind. It’s more vague than that, if it’s there at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has gone quiet. Like I still do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s mostly obligations.\n\nI used to get something out of my work, and yoga, and going to galleries—there’s this small gallery near me that used to change the whole feel of my week—and even journaling. Now I just don’t really feel drawn toward any of it. It’s like I’m moving through the motions, and I don’t know, that’s been hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and everything shifted—space, routines, even the way I think about work and people. I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment, you know, like when furniture is in the wrong place and you keep bumping into it, but it’s been… yeah, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like spending time with friends or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.\n\nAnd with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that. Sometimes I get tired in this heavy way, like I wish I could just not have to deal with everything for a while, but that’s different, I think.\n\nIt’s funny, “death” makes me think of this client I had years ago who used to talk around it without ever saying the word, and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what they meant—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess… no clear thoughts of wanting to die that I’ve noticed. More like wanting things to pause.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but that’s not really the thing I notice most. I mean, sometimes I’m slower getting started, like I’ll sit with my journal open and not write anything, or I’ll take forever to leave for yoga even though I know the class time.\n\nBut what finally made me come in is more that the part of me that used to reach for people, and enjoy my work, and yoga, and galleries… that part feels very quiet. I’m still doing things, mostly because they’re obligations or routines, but I don’t feel pulled toward them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. My scale at home is one of those old ones that kind of gives a different answer depending on where you stand, which is annoying—I bought it years ago after a client was talking about calorie counting and it made me think I should be more “aware,” but then it just became another object in the bathroom.\n\nSorry. In terms of weight, I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting very differently or anything dramatic. So… no significant loss that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.\n\nI do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe not hopeless, but I don’t have a very clear feeling of looking forward right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is worse than your feelings of sadness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.\n\nIt’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. Like I’m still showing up to things because they’re on the calendar, or because someone expects me to, but it feels like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting them.\n\nI remember I used to plan weekends around exhibits, and I’d have opinions about the lighting or the way things were hung—probably too many opinions, honestly. Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So yes, I’d say the lack of interest is more noticeable than sadness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A few, maybe, but not in the way I used to. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. Since then everything has felt… different.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. So… yes, I’d say it’s been getting worse enough that I noticed I’m mostly just moving through obligations now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. Not about one clear thing, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I can feel bad that I’m not as present as I used to be, especially with people who probably expect me to show up in a certain way. But then I start thinking about how much of my work has always been about being present for other people, and how strange it is when you can do that professionally—or at least try to—and then your own life feels sort of… unattended.\n\nBut guilt about something specific? No, nothing really comes to mind. It’s more vague than that, if it’s there at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about the clothes specifically… I haven’t really been tracking it that way. The bigger thing I can place is that this whole shift—how I’m thinking, how I’m working, how I’m connecting with people—it’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Or not started exactly like a switch flipped, but that’s the point I keep circling back to when I try to make sense of the timing. Since then everything has felt… different.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… I feel like the part of me that used to reach for people has gone quiet. Like I still do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s mostly obligations.\n\nI used to get something out of my work, and yoga, and going to galleries—there’s this small gallery near me that used to change the whole feel of my week—and even journaling. Now I just don’t really feel drawn toward any of it. It’s like I’m moving through the motions, and I don’t know, that’s been hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and everything shifted—space, routines, even the way I think about work and people. I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment, you know, like when furniture is in the wrong place and you keep bumping into it, but it’s been… yeah, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like spending time with friends or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.\n\nAnd with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that. Sometimes I get tired in this heavy way, like I wish I could just not have to deal with everything for a while, but that’s different, I think.\n\nIt’s funny, “death” makes me think of this client I had years ago who used to talk around it without ever saying the word, and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what they meant—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess… no clear thoughts of wanting to die that I’ve noticed. More like wanting things to pause.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but that’s not really the thing I notice most. I mean, sometimes I’m slower getting started, like I’ll sit with my journal open and not write anything, or I’ll take forever to leave for yoga even though I know the class time.\n\nBut what finally made me come in is more that the part of me that used to reach for people, and enjoy my work, and yoga, and galleries… that part feels very quiet. I’m still doing things, mostly because they’re obligations or routines, but I don’t feel pulled toward them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. My scale at home is one of those old ones that kind of gives a different answer depending on where you stand, which is annoying—I bought it years ago after a client was talking about calorie counting and it made me think I should be more “aware,” but then it just became another object in the bathroom.\n\nSorry. In terms of weight, I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting very differently or anything dramatic. So… no significant loss that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.\n\nI do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe not hopeless, but I don’t have a very clear feeling of looking forward right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is worse than your feelings of sadness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.\n\nIt’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. Like I’m still showing up to things because they’re on the calendar, or because someone expects me to, but it feels like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting them.\n\nI remember I used to plan weekends around exhibits, and I’d have opinions about the lighting or the way things were hung—probably too many opinions, honestly. Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So yes, I’d say the lack of interest is more noticeable than sadness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A few, maybe, but not in the way I used to. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.\n\nIt’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. Like I’m still showing up to things because they’re on the calendar, or because someone expects me to, but it feels like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting them.\n\nI remember I used to plan weekends around exhibits, and I’d have opinions about the lighting or the way things were hung—probably too many opinions, honestly. Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So yes, I’d say the lack of interest is more noticeable than sadness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A few, maybe, but not in the way I used to. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. So… yes, I’d say it’s been getting worse enough that I noticed I’m mostly just moving through obligations now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. Not about one clear thing, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I can feel bad that I’m not as present as I used to be, especially with people who probably expect me to show up in a certain way. But then I start thinking about how much of my work has always been about being present for other people, and how strange it is when you can do that professionally—or at least try to—and then your own life feels sort of… unattended.\n\nBut guilt about something specific? No, nothing really comes to mind. It’s more vague than that, if it’s there at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about the clothes specifically… I haven’t really been tracking it that way. The bigger thing I can place is that this whole shift—how I’m thinking, how I’m working, how I’m connecting with people—it’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Or not started exactly like a switch flipped, but that’s the point I keep circling back to when I try to make sense of the timing. Since then everything has felt… different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had anyone comment on your weight loss over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. No one has said, “You’ve lost weight,” or anything direct like that.\n\nPeople comment on appearance in such odd ways anyway—like “you look tired” can mean ten different things, and with clients I used to be careful not to read too much into those little observations. But about weight specifically, no, I don’t think anyone has mentioned it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, maybe… but not the way I used to.

I used to have these things I naturally reached for—my work, yoga, going to galleries, even journaling. They weren’t just tasks, they felt like parts of me. And now it’s more like I know those things are supposed to matter, but the feeling behind them has gone very quiet.

I’ll still do things sometimes because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to, but it feels more like moving through obligations than enjoying them. That’s actually part of why I finally came in, I think. Something in me that used to connect to people and to those activities just… isn’t really reaching anymore.

Now I just don’t really feel drawn toward any of it. It’s like I’m moving through the motions, and I don’t know, that’s been hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It kind of started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and everything shifted—space, routines, even the way I think about work and people. I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment, you know, like when furniture is in the wrong place and you keep bumping into it, but it’s been… yeah, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like spending time with friends or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.\n\nAnd with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that. I mean, sometimes I’m slower getting started, like I’ll sit with my journal open and not write anything, or I’ll take forever to leave for yoga even though I know the class time.\n\nBut what finally made me come in is more that the part of me that used to reach for people, and enjoy my work, and yoga, and galleries… that part feels very quiet. I’m still doing things, mostly because they’re obligations or routines, but I don’t feel pulled toward them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. My scale at home is one of those old ones that kind of gives a different answer depending on where you stand, which is annoying—I bought it years ago after a client was talking about calorie counting and it made me think I should be more “aware,” but then it just became another object in the bathroom.\n\nSorry. In terms of weight, I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting very differently or anything dramatic. So… no significant loss that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.\n\nI do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe not hopeless, but I don’t have a very clear feeling of looking forward right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is worse than your feelings of sadness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.\n\nIt’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. Like I’m still showing up to things because they’re on the calendar, or because someone expects me to, but it feels like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting them.\n\nI remember I used to plan weekends around exhibits, and I’d have opinions about the lighting or the way things were hung—probably too many opinions, honestly. Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So yes, I’d say the lack of interest is more noticeable than sadness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A few, maybe, but not in the way I used to. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. So… yes, I’d say it’s been getting worse enough that I noticed I’m mostly just moving through obligations now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. Not about one clear thing, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I can feel bad that I’m not as present as I used to be, especially with people who probably expect me to show up in a certain way. But then I start thinking about how much of my work has always been about being present for other people, and how strange it is when you can do that professionally—or at least try to—and then your own life feels sort of… unattended.\n\nBut guilt about something specific? No, nothing really comes to mind. It’s more vague than that, if it’s there at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about the clothes specifically… I haven’t really been tracking it that way. The bigger thing I can place is that this whole shift—how I’m thinking, how I’m working, how I’m connecting with people—it’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Or not started exactly like a switch flipped, but that’s the point I keep circling back to when I try to make sense of the timing. Since then everything has felt… different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had anyone comment on your weight loss over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. No one has said, “You’ve lost weight,” or anything direct like that.\n\nPeople comment on appearance in such odd ways anyway—like “you look tired” can mean ten different things, and with clients I used to be careful not to read too much into those little observations. But about weight specifically, no, I don’t think anyone has mentioned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find you're still able to enjoy, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not the way I used to.\n\nI used to have these things I naturally reached for—my work, yoga, going to galleries, even journaling. They weren’t just tasks, they felt like parts of me. And now it’s more like I know those things are supposed to matter, but the feeling behind them has gone very quiet.\n\nI’ll still do things sometimes because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to, but it feels more like moving through obligations than enjoying them. That’s actually part of why I finally came in, I think. Something in me that used to connect to people and to those activities just… isn’t really reaching anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I mean, I’m still going to work, so part of me wants to say I’m functioning. But it’s not at my usual level.

Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So… yes, I’d say it’s been getting worse enough that I noticed I’m mostly just moving through obligations now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. Not about one clear thing, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I can feel bad that I’m not as present as I used to be, especially with people who probably expect me to show up in a certain way. But then I start thinking about how much of my work has always been about being present for other people, and how strange it is when you can do that professionally—or at least try to—and then your own life feels sort of… unattended.\n\nBut guilt about something specific? No, nothing really comes to mind. It’s more vague than that, if it’s there at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about the clothes specifically… I haven’t really been tracking it that way. The bigger thing I can place is that this whole shift—how I’m thinking, how I’m working, how I’m connecting with people—it’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Or not started exactly like a switch flipped, but that’s the point I keep circling back to when I try to make sense of the timing. Since then everything has felt… different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had anyone comment on your weight loss over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. No one has said, “You’ve lost weight,” or anything direct like that.\n\nPeople comment on appearance in such odd ways anyway—like “you look tired” can mean ten different things, and with clients I used to be careful not to read too much into those little observations. But about weight specifically, no, I don’t think anyone has mentioned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find you're still able to enjoy, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not the way I used to.\n\nI used to have these things I naturally reached for—my work, yoga, going to galleries, even journaling. They weren’t just tasks, they felt like parts of me. And now it’s more like I know those things are supposed to matter, but the feeling behind them has gone very quiet.\n\nI’ll still do things sometimes because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to, but it feels more like moving through obligations than enjoying them. That’s actually part of why I finally came in, I think. Something in me that used to connect to people and to those activities just… isn’t really reaching anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is significantly impacting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still going to work, so part of me wants to say I’m functioning. But it’s not at my usual level.\n\nI’ve missed a couple of grant deadlines, which isn’t like me. I used to be the person reminding everyone else about those things—color-coded folders, little sticky notes everywhere, the whole system. And now I look at the paperwork and it just sort of… sits there.\n\nMy supervisor hasn’t made a big dramatic thing of it, but I can tell. Some of my more demanding clients have been moved to other clinicians, quietly. She said it in a very practical way, like it was just caseload balancing, but I know what it means. So yes, it is affecting my daily life, especially at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure exactly what you mean by “thoughts about death.” Like… in a general way, everyone sort of thinks about mortality sometimes, especially getting older, seeing parents age, that kind of thing.\n\nBut if you mean something more specific, like sitting around thinking about death a lot, I don’t think so. Yes… I think it is.

Not in a dramatic way, like some huge argument happened. It’s more that I don’t reach out. I used to be better about texting people back, making plans, going to openings with friends, even just having that little thread of interest in what everyone was doing.

I kept telling myself it was just an adjustment, you know, like when furniture is in the wrong place and you keep bumping into it, but it’s been… yeah, about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like spending time with friends or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do some of them because they’re on the calendar or because I know I’m “supposed” to, but the enjoyment part feels… muted.\n\nLike with yoga, I’ll go through the poses, but it doesn’t settle me the way it used to. And the galleries—I used to really look forward to that, even just wandering around, noticing colors or weird little details. Lately it’s more like I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to pick up groceries after.\n\nAnd with people… I don’t know, I used to reach out more. Now that part of me feels quiet. Work too—I care in an obligation way, not in the way I used to. It’s hard to explain, but it’s like I’m still doing the outline of my life without feeling much pleasure in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve been sitting around making plans or anything like that. Sometimes I get tired in this heavy way, like I wish I could just not have to deal with everything for a while, but that’s different, I think.\n\nIt’s funny, “death” makes me think of this client I had years ago who used to talk around it without ever saying the word, and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what they meant—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess… no clear thoughts of wanting to die that I’ve noticed. More like wanting things to pause.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but that’s not really the thing I notice most. I mean, sometimes I’m slower getting started, like I’ll sit with my journal open and not write anything, or I’ll take forever to leave for yoga even though I know the class time.\n\nBut what finally made me come in is more that the part of me that used to reach for people, and enjoy my work, and yoga, and galleries… that part feels very quiet. I’m still doing things, mostly because they’re obligations or routines, but I don’t feel pulled toward them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. My scale at home is one of those old ones that kind of gives a different answer depending on where you stand, which is annoying—I bought it years ago after a client was talking about calorie counting and it made me think I should be more “aware,” but then it just became another object in the bathroom.\n\nSorry. In terms of weight, I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting very differently or anything dramatic. So… no significant loss that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. That word feels very final, like closing a door. I don’t think I’d say I’m hopeless exactly.\n\nI do have these moments where the future feels kind of blank, like when people ask, “Where do you see yourself in five years?” and I just think of those career workshops where they make you draw a little roadmap—always hated those, honestly, they felt so artificial. I used to tell clients not to worry if they couldn’t picture everything, and now I’m sitting here doing the same thing.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe not hopeless, but I don’t have a very clear feeling of looking forward right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is worse than your feelings of sadness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, sadness is there sometimes, but it’s not like I’m crying all day or feeling this huge wave of grief.\n\nIt’s more that the part of me that used to reach for things—people, work, yoga, going to galleries, even just sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. Like I’m still showing up to things because they’re on the calendar, or because someone expects me to, but it feels like I’m moving through obligations more than actually wanting them.\n\nI remember I used to plan weekends around exhibits, and I’d have opinions about the lighting or the way things were hung—probably too many opinions, honestly. Now I can look at the same kind of thing and it just doesn’t pull me in. So yes, I’d say the lack of interest is more noticeable than sadness.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: A few, maybe, but not in the way I used to. Like… I still go to yoga sometimes, and I’ll still go to a gallery if someone invites me or if it’s already planned. But it’s more like I’m following through because that’s what I’m supposed to do.\n\nI used to really look forward to those things. I’d journal, I’d think about my clients, I’d feel engaged with the work—well, the work could be draining, obviously, social work is not exactly a light hobby—but there was meaning in it. Now it’s like that part of me that reached for people and activities has gotten very quiet.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is… not much. I mostly just move through obligations.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been getting worse, or maybe more noticeable. It’s hard to tell because time gets sort of smeared together when you’re just doing what’s on the calendar.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is that the part of me that used to reach for people—like checking in on friends, caring about work, wanting to go to yoga or a gallery, even sitting down to journal—has gone really quiet. I still do some of it, but it feels like I’m fulfilling a duty more than actually wanting to be there.\n\nI keep thinking about how I used to tell clients that small routines can carry you for a while, and I suppose they can, but it’s strange when your own routines start feeling like empty boxes you’re checking off. So… yes, I’d say it’s been getting worse enough that I noticed I’m mostly just moving through obligations now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure. Not about one clear thing, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I can feel bad that I’m not as present as I used to be, especially with people who probably expect me to show up in a certain way. But then I start thinking about how much of my work has always been about being present for other people, and how strange it is when you can do that professionally—or at least try to—and then your own life feels sort of… unattended.\n\nBut guilt about something specific? No, nothing really comes to mind. It’s more vague than that, if it’s there at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how your clothes fit over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about the clothes specifically… I haven’t really been tracking it that way. The bigger thing I can place is that this whole shift—how I’m thinking, how I’m working, how I’m connecting with people—it’s been about eight months now.\n\nIt started around when we took my older relative into our apartment. Or not started exactly like a switch flipped, but that’s the point I keep circling back to when I try to make sense of the timing. Since then everything has felt… different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had anyone comment on your weight loss over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. No one has said, “You’ve lost weight,” or anything direct like that.\n\nPeople comment on appearance in such odd ways anyway—like “you look tired” can mean ten different things, and with clients I used to be careful not to read too much into those little observations. But about weight specifically, no, I don’t think anyone has mentioned it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find you're still able to enjoy, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but not the way I used to.\n\nI used to have these things I naturally reached for—my work, yoga, going to galleries, even journaling. They weren’t just tasks, they felt like parts of me. And now it’s more like I know those things are supposed to matter, but the feeling behind them has gone very quiet.\n\nI’ll still do things sometimes because they’re on the calendar or because someone expects me to, but it feels more like moving through obligations than enjoying them. That’s actually part of why I finally came in, I think. Something in me that used to connect to people and to those activities just… isn’t really reaching anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is significantly impacting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still going to work, so part of me wants to say I’m functioning. But it’s not at my usual level.\n\nI’ve missed a couple of grant deadlines, which isn’t like me. I used to be the person reminding everyone else about those things—color-coded folders, little sticky notes everywhere, the whole system. And now I look at the paperwork and it just sort of… sits there.\n\nMy supervisor hasn’t made a big dramatic thing of it, but I can tell. Some of my more demanding clients have been moved to other clinicians, quietly. She said it in a very practical way, like it was just caseload balancing, but I know what it means. So yes, it is affecting my daily life, especially at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure exactly what you mean by “thoughts about death.” Like… in a general way, everyone sort of thinks about mortality sometimes, especially getting older, seeing parents age, that kind of thing.\n\nBut if you mean something more specific, like sitting around thinking about death a lot, I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that as a theme, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is interfering with your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is.\n\nNot in a dramatic way, like some huge argument happened. It’s more that I don’t reach out. I used to be better about texting people back, making plans, going to openings with friends, even just having that little thread of interest in what everyone was doing.\n\nNow I’ll see a message and think, I should answer that, and then somehow it becomes three days later. Or I go because I said I would, but I’m sort of just… present in the room, not really with people.\n\nIt’s the same feeling I was trying to describe before—the part of me that used to take pleasure in work, yoga, galleries, journaling, people… it’s gone very quiet. I’m mostly just moving through what I’m supposed to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizophrenia, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is significantly impacting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still going to work, so part of me wants to say I’m functioning. But it’s not at my usual level.\n\nI’ve missed a couple of grant deadlines, which isn’t like me. I used to be the person reminding everyone else about those things—color-coded folders, little sticky notes everywhere, the whole system. And now I look at the paperwork and it just sort of… sits there.\n\nMy supervisor hasn’t made a big dramatic thing of it, but I can tell. Some of my more demanding clients have been moved to other clinicians, quietly. She said it in a very practical way, like it was just caseload balancing, but I know what it means. So yes, it is affecting my daily life, especially at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure exactly what you mean by “thoughts about death.” Like… in a general way, everyone sort of thinks about mortality sometimes, especially getting older, seeing parents age, that kind of thing.\n\nBut if you mean something more specific, like sitting around thinking about death a lot, I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that as a theme, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest in activities is interfering with your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is.\n\nNot in a dramatic way, like some huge argument happened. It’s more that I don’t reach out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not a noticeable amount, anyway.

I mean, I don’t weigh myself that often. I used to, back when I was doing yoga more regularly—there was this whole period where everyone at the studio was talking about “wellness metrics,” which honestly got a little obsessive, so I stopped keeping track like that.

My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything. So… no, I haven’t noticed a significant weight loss.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
